Var.count\_observations() → int[](#keysight.pwdatatools.Var.count_observations "Link to this definition")
:   Return the number of observations in this Var.

    The number of observations is equal to the size of the data along
    axis 0.

    Return type:
    :   *int*

<!-- === 来源: api_reference/main/var/_autosummary/keysight.pwdatatools.Var.dims.md === -->

# keysight.pwdatatools.Var.dims[](#keysight-pwdatatools-var-dims "Link to this heading")

*property* Var.dims*: [Dims](../../../metadata/dims/index.md#keysight.pwdatatools.Dims "keysight.pwdatatools._core.variables.dims.Dims")*[](#keysight.pwdatatools.Var.dims "Link to this definition")
:   The variable’s metadata related to dimensions.

    It is an instance of [`Dims`](../../../metadata/dims/index.md#keysight.pwdatatools.Dims "keysight.pwdatatools.Dims"), which can store dimension
    names and scales for each of the variable’s higher dimensions.
    It is always consistent with the shape of the variable’s data.

    Examples

    A 1D Var always has empty Dims because there are no higher
    dimensions for a 1D Var, and [`Dims`](../../../metadata/dims/index.md#keysight.pwdatatools.Dims "keysight.pwdatatools.Dims") only stores metadata
    for higher dimensions.

    ```
    >>> from keysight.pwdatatools import Var
    >>> x = Var([10, 12, 13], name='x')
    >>> x.dims
    Dims(ndim=1)
    >>> x.has_empty_dims()
    True
    ```

    Initializing a 2D Var or 3D Var without explicitly providing a
    dims argument also results in empty Dims, just like the 1D case.

    ```
    >>> import numpy as np
    >>> data_2d = np.random.random(6).reshape((3, 2))
    >>> var_2d = Var(data_2d, name='var_2d')
    >>> var_2d.dims
    Dims(ndim=2)
    >>> var_2d.has_empty_dims()
    True
    >>> data_3d = np.random.random(20).reshape((5, 2, 2))
    >>> var_3d = Var(data_3d, name='var_3d')
    >>> var_3d.dims
    Dims(ndim=3)
    >>> var_3d.has_empty_dims()
    True
    ```

    Now, let’s initialize a 2D Var with dims that include i\_names,
    which enables indexing along the “i” dimension using strings
    instead of integers.

    ```
    >>> from keysight.pwdatatools import Dims
    >>> import numpy as np
    >>> dims = Dims(ndim=2, i_names=['P1', 'P2'])
    >>> var_2d_dims = Var(data_2d, name='var_2d_dims', dims=dims)
    >>> var_2d_dims.dims
    Dims(
        ndim=2,
        i_nums=None,
        i_names=['P1', 'P2'],
    )
    ```

    Below, we initialize a 3D Var with dims that include i\_nums,
    which enables indexing along the “i” and “j” dimensions using
    arbitrary integers, rather than just 0-based or 1-based integers.

    ```
    >>> dims = Dims(ndim=3, i_nums=[3, 4], j_nums=[7, 8])
    >>> var_3d_dims = Var(data_3d, name='s', dims=dims)
    >>> var_3d_dims.dims
    Dims(
        ndim=3,
        i_nums=[3, 4],
        i_names=None,
        j_nums=[7, 8],
        j_names=None,
    )
    ```


---

<!-- === 来源: api_reference/main/var/_autosummary/keysight.pwdatatools.Var.drop_observations.md === -->

# keysight.pwdatatools.Var.drop\_observations[](#keysight-pwdatatools-var-drop-observations "Link to this heading")

Var.drop\_observations(*condition: ArrayLike*) → Self[](#keysight.pwdatatools.Var.drop_observations "Link to this definition")
:   Return a new Var with points along axis 0 removed where condition is True.

    Parameters:
    :   **condition** (*bool array-like*) – A boolean array-like of the same length as the Var’s data
        along axis 0.

    Returns:
    :   A new Var with the same name, attrs, role, and unit as
        this Var, but with a new data array and possibly new dims.

    Return type:
    :   [*Var*](../index.md#keysight.pwdatatools.Var "keysight.pwdatatools.Var")

    Examples

    ```
    >>> from keysight.pwdatatools import Var
    >>> gain = Var([10, 11, 12, 13, 14], name='Gain')
    >>> gain_filtered = gain.drop_observations([True, False, False, True, False])
    >>> gain_filtered.to_numpy_ndarray()
    array([11, 12, 14])
    ```

    Now let’s do the same thing, but with a 2D Var.

    ```
    >>> import numpy as np
    >>> data = np.arange(20).reshape((5, 4))
    >>> portz = Var(data, name='PortZ')
    >>> portz_filtered = portz.drop_observations([True, False, False, True, False])
    >>> portz_filtered.to_numpy_ndarray()
    array([[ 4,  5,  6,  7],
           [ 8,  9, 10, 11],
           [16, 17, 18, 19]])
    ```


---

<!-- === 来源: api_reference/main/var/_autosummary/keysight.pwdatatools.Var.dtype.md === -->

*property* Var.dtype*: [DataType](../../../public_submodules/datatypes/_autosummary/keysight.pwdatatools.datatypes.DataType.md#keysight.pwdatatools.datatypes.DataType "keysight.pwdatatools._core.datatypes.DataType")*[](#keysight.pwdatatools.Var.dtype "Link to this definition")

<!-- === 来源: api_reference/main/var/_autosummary/keysight.pwdatatools.Var.fill_nan.md === -->

# keysight.pwdatatools.Var.fill\_nan[](#keysight-pwdatatools-var-fill-nan "Link to this heading")

Var.fill\_nan(*value: Scalar*) → Self[](#keysight.pwdatatools.Var.fill_nan "Link to this definition")
:   Return a new Var with NaNs filled with the given value.

    Parameters:
    :   **value** (*scalar*) – The value used to fill NaNs.

    Returns:
    :   A new Var with the same name, attrs, role, and unit as
        this Var, but with a new data array and possibly new dims.

    Return type:
    :   [*Var*](../index.md#keysight.pwdatatools.Var "keysight.pwdatatools.Var")

    Examples

    ```
    >>> import numpy as np
    >>> from keysight.pwdatatools import Var
    >>> data = np.array([1, np.nan, 3])
    >>> v = Var(data, name='v')
    >>> v_filled = v.fill_nan(42)
    >>> v_filled.to_numpy_ndarray()
    array([ 1., 42.,  3.])
    ```


---

<!-- === 来源: api_reference/main/var/_autosummary/keysight.pwdatatools.Var.fill_null.md === -->

# keysight.pwdatatools.Var.fill\_null[](#keysight-pwdatatools-var-fill-null "Link to this heading")

Var.fill\_null(*value: Scalar*) → Self[](#keysight.pwdatatools.Var.fill_null "Link to this definition")
:   Return a new Var with nulls filled with the given value.

    Parameters:
    :   **value** (*scalar*) – The value used to fill nulls.

    Returns:
    :   A new Var with the same name, attrs, role, and unit as
        this Var, but with a new data array and possibly new dims.

    Return type:
    :   [*Var*](../index.md#keysight.pwdatatools.Var "keysight.pwdatatools.Var")

    Examples

    ```
    >>> import numpy as np
    >>> from keysight.pwdatatools import Var
    >>> data = np.ma.array([1, 2, 3], mask=[False, True, False])
    >>> v = Var(data, name='v')
    >>> v_filled = v.fill_null(42)
    >>> v_filled.to_numpy_ndarray()
    array([ 1, 42,  3])
    ```

# keysight.pwdatatools.Var.from\_1D\_vars[](#keysight-pwdatatools-var-from-1d-vars "Link to this heading")

*classmethod* Var.from\_1D\_vars(*vars1D: Iterable['Var']*, */*, *\**, *name: str*, *dims: DimsLike | None = None*) → [Var](../index.md#keysight.pwdatatools.Var "keysight.pwdatatools.Var")[](#keysight.pwdatatools.Var.from_1D_vars "Link to this definition")
:   Make a multi-dimensional Var by combining 1D Vars.

    :   * **vars1D** (*Iterable of Var*) – Iterable of 1D Vars to combine.
        * **name** (*str*) – Name of the new Var.
        * **dims** (*DimsLike, optional*) – Deifnes the dims for the new multi-dim Var.

    :   [*Var*](../index.md#keysight.pwdatatools.Var "keysight.pwdatatools.Var")

<!-- === 来源: api_reference/main/var/_autosummary/keysight.pwdatatools.Var.from_pandas_dataframe.md === -->

# keysight.pwdatatools.Var.from\_pandas\_dataframe[](#keysight-pwdatatools-var-from-pandas-dataframe "Link to this heading")

*classmethod* Var.from\_pandas\_dataframe(*dataframe: pd.DataFrame*, */*, *\**, *has\_varname: bool = True*, *name: str | None = None*, *dtype: NumpyDtypeLike = None*, *dimscales\_delim: Tuple[str, str, str] = ('[', ',', ']')*) → [Var](../index.md#keysight.pwdatatools.Var "keysight.pwdatatools.Var")[](#keysight.pwdatatools.Var.from_pandas_dataframe "Link to this definition")
:   Create a Var from a pandas DataFrame.

    Parameters:
    :   * **dataframe** (*pandas.DataFrame*) – The DataFrame to convert to a Var.
        * **has\_varname** (*bool, default True*) – Set this parameter to `True` if the DataFrame’s columns
          contain the variable’s name. The variable’s name may be
          in the first level of MultiIndex columns, or as part of a flat
          column index. If `False`, the DataFrame’s columns must not
          contain the variable’s name.
        * **name** (*str, optional*) – An optional name of the returned Var. If provided, it takes
          precedence over the varname in the DataFrame’s columns.
          Otherwise, the Var’s name is obtained from the DataFrame’s
          columns.
        * **dtype** (*dtype-like, optional*) – The dtype of the Var’s data ndarray. If `None`, the dtype is
          inferred from the dtypes in the DataFrame.
        * **dimscales\_delim** (*Tuple[str, str, str], default (‘[’, ‘,’, ‘]’)*) – A tuple of str characters for embedding dimension scales into
          the labels of a flat string columns index. Defaults to
          `('[', ',', ']')`. The first character is the opening
          delimiter, the second is the delimiter between dimension
          scales, and the third is the closing delimiter. For example,
          the default delimiters would work for labels like
          `'S[1,1]'`, `'S[1,2]'`, etc.

    Return type:
    :   [*Var*](../index.md#keysight.pwdatatools.Var "keysight.pwdatatools.Var")

    Raises:
    :   **ValueError** –

        * If `has_varname=True` and the DataFrame’s columns do
          not appear to contain the variable’s name.
          - If `has_varname=False` and `name=None`.

    Notes

    The Var’s unit, role, and attrs are always be empty. If the
    DataFrame has an unnamed, zero-based integer index, the Var’s first
    Dim will be empty. Otherwise, the first Dim’s name and scale are
    derived from the DataFrame’s index. The Var’s remaining dims are
    derived from the DataFrame’s columns.


---

<!-- === 来源: api_reference/main/var/_autosummary/keysight.pwdatatools.Var.from_pandas_series.md === -->

# keysight.pwdatatools.Var.from\_pandas\_series[](#keysight-pwdatatools-var-from-pandas-series "Link to this heading")

*classmethod* Var.from\_pandas\_series(*series: pd.Series*, */*, *\**, *name: str | None = None*, *dtype: NumpyDtypeLike = None*) → [Var](../index.md#keysight.pwdatatools.Var "keysight.pwdatatools.Var")[](#keysight.pwdatatools.Var.from_pandas_series "Link to this definition")
:   Create a Var from a pandas Series.

    The Var’s name is set by the name input arg, not by the input
    `series.name` attribute. The Var’s dims are set to
    `None` if the Series has an unnamed zero-based integer index.
    The Var’s counts, unit, role, and attrs are always
    `None`.

    Parameters:
    :   * **series** (*pandas.Series*) – The pandas Series to convert to a Var.
        * **name** (*str, optional*) – Optional name of the new Var. If provided, the name of the
          input Series is ignored. If not provded, the name of the input
          Series is used.
        * **dtype** (*dtype-like, optional*) – The dtype of the Var’s data ndarray. If `None`, the dtype is
          inferred from the dtype of the input Series.

    Return type:
    :   [*Var*](../index.md#keysight.pwdatatools.Var "keysight.pwdatatools.Var")


---

<!-- === 来源: api_reference/main/var/_autosummary/keysight.pwdatatools.Var.has_empty_dims.md === -->

# keysight.pwdatatools.Var.has\_empty\_dims[](#keysight-pwdatatools-var-has-empty-dims "Link to this heading")

Var.has\_empty\_dims() → bool[](#keysight.pwdatatools.Var.has_empty_dims "Link to this definition")
:   Return bool indicating whether this Var has empty dims.

    Empty dims are defined as Dims having no scales.

    Return type:
    :   *bool*

    Examples

    Let’s illustrate with a 1D Var.

    ```
    >>> from keysight.pwdatatools import Var
    >>> gain = Var([10, 12, 13], name='Gain')
    >>> gain.dims
    DimsTuple(
        <empty Dim>,
    )
    >>> gain.has_empty_dims()
    True
    ```

    Give the Dim a non-default name.

    ```
    >>> gain.dims[0].name = 'foo'
    >>> gain.dims
    DimsTuple(
        <Dim 'foo' with no scales>,
    )
    >>> gain.has_empty_dims()
    False
    ```

    Now, let’s demonstrate with a 3D Var.

    ```
    >>> import numpy as np
    >>> from keysight.pwdatatools import Dim, DimScale, Var
    >>> data = np.random.random(20).reshape((5, 2, 2))
    >>> s = Var(data, name='S')
    >>> s.dims
    DimsTuple(
        <empty Dim>,
        <Dim 'i' with no scales>,
        <Dim 'j' with no scales>,
    )
    >>> s.has_empty_dims()
    True
    ```

    Give the Dims non-default scales.

    ```
    >>> dims = (
    ...     Dim(),
    ...     Dim(name='i', scales=DimScale([1, 2], name='portnum')),
    ...     Dim(name='j', scales=DimScale([1, 2], name='portnum'))
    ... )
    >>> s = Var(data, name='S', dims=dims)
    >>> s.dims
    DimsTuple(
        <empty Dim>,
        <Dim 'i' with 1 scale>,
        <Dim 'j' with 1 scale>,
    )
    >>> s.has_empty_dims()
    False
    ```


---

<!-- === 来源: api_reference/main/var/_autosummary/keysight.pwdatatools.Var.has_role.md === -->

# keysight.pwdatatools.Var.has\_role[](#keysight-pwdatatools-var-has-role "Link to this heading")

Var.has\_role(*role: str*, *exact: bool = False*) → bool[](#keysight.pwdatatools.Var.has_role "Link to this definition")
:   Return bool indicating whether this Var has the given role.

    Parameters:
    :   * **role** (*str*) – The role to match.
        * **exact** (*bool, default False*) – If `False`, the role is considered a match if it is either
          a subrole or an exact match of an existing role. If `True`,
          the role is considered a match only if it is an exact match
          of an existing role. Even if `True`, the role is finalized
          before testing for a match (auxiliary roles are sorted, etc.).

    Return type:
    :   *bool*

    Examples

    ```
    >>> from keysight.pwdatatools import Var
    >>> gain = Var([10, 12, 13], name='Gain', role='gain.power')
    ```

    The default behavior is to match to any subrole or exact role.

    ```
    >>> gain.has_role('gain')
    True
    >>> gain.has_role('gain.operating')
    True
    >>> gain.has_role('gain.power')
    True
    ```

    If `exact=True`, only exact matches are considered, and subrole matches
    are not considered.

    ```
    >>> gain.has_role('gain', exact=True)
    False
    >>> gain.has_role('gain.power', exact=True)
    True
    ```


---

Var.is\_nan() → NDArray[np.bool\_][](#keysight.pwdatatools.Var.is_nan "Link to this definition")

Var.is\_null() → NDArray[np.bool\_][](#keysight.pwdatatools.Var.is_null "Link to this definition")

<!-- === 来源: api_reference/main/var/_autosummary/keysight.pwdatatools.Var.keep_observations.md === -->

# keysight.pwdatatools.Var.keep\_observations[](#keysight-pwdatatools-var-keep-observations "Link to this heading")

Var.keep\_observations(*condition: ArrayLike*) → Self[](#keysight.pwdatatools.Var.keep_observations "Link to this definition")
:   Return a new Var with observations along axis 0 kept where condition is True.

    Parameters:
    :   **condition** (*bool array-like*) – A boolean array-like of the same length as the Var’s data
        along axis 0.

    Returns:
    :   A new Var with the same name, attrs, role, and unit as
        this Var, but with a new data array and possibly new dims.

    Return type:
    :   [*Var*](../index.md#keysight.pwdatatools.Var "keysight.pwdatatools.Var")

    Examples

    ```
    >>> from keysight.pwdatatools import Var
    >>> gain = Var([10, 11, 12, 13, 14], name='Gain')
    >>> gain_filtered = gain.keep_observations([True, False, False, True, False])
    >>> gain_filtered.to_numpy_ndarray()
    array([10, 13])
    ```

    Now let’s do the same thing, but with a 2D Var.

    ```
    >>> import numpy as np
    >>> data = np.arange(20).reshape((5, 4))
    >>> portz = Var(data, name='PortZ')
    >>> portz_filtered = portz.keep_observations([True, False, False, True, False])
    >>> portz_filtered.to_numpy_ndarray()
    array([[ 0,  1,  2,  3],
           [12, 13, 14, 15]])
    ```

# keysight.pwdatatools.Var.rename[](#keysight-pwdatatools-var-rename "Link to this heading")

Var.rename(*name: str*) → Self[](#keysight.pwdatatools.Var.rename "Link to this definition")
:   Return a new Var instance with the new name.

    Parameters:
    :   **name** (*str*) – The new Var’s name.

    Returns:
    :   A new Var instance with the new name.

    Return type:
    :   [*Var*](../index.md#keysight.pwdatatools.Var "keysight.pwdatatools.Var")

    Examples

    ```
    >>> import numpy as np
    >>> from keysight.pwdatatools import Var
    >>> vout = Var([1, 2, 3], name='Vout', role='voltage', unit='V')
    >>> vout2 = vout.rename('Vout2')
    >>> vout2.name
    'Vout2'
    >>> vout.name

    ```

# keysight.pwdatatools.Var.repeat\_observations[](#keysight-pwdatatools-var-repeat-observations "Link to this heading")

Var.repeat\_observations(*counts: NDArray[np.integer]*) → Self[](#keysight.pwdatatools.Var.repeat_observations "Link to this definition")
:   Return new Var with obervations along axis 0 repeated according to counts.

    Parameters:

        along axis 0. Each integer is the number of times to repeat
        the corresponding point in the Var’s data.

    Returns:

    :   [*Var*](../index.md#keysight.pwdatatools.Var "keysight.pwdatatools.Var")

<!-- === 来源: api_reference/main/var/_autosummary/keysight.pwdatatools.Var.replace.md === -->

# keysight.pwdatatools.Var.replace[](#keysight-pwdatatools-var-replace "Link to this heading")

Var.replace(*\*\*kwargs*) → Self[](#keysight.pwdatatools.Var.replace "Link to this definition")
:   Return a new Var with specified data and/or metadata replaced with new values.

    Parameters:
    :   **\*\*kwargs** – The new values to replace. Any of the following may be provided:
        - `data`: The new data.
        - `name`: The new name.
        - `attrs`: The new attrs.
        - `dims`: The new dims.
        - `role`: The new role.
        - `unit`: The new unit.

    Returns:
    :   A new Var instance with the new data and/or metadata.

    Return type:
    :   [*Var*](../index.md#keysight.pwdatatools.Var "keysight.pwdatatools.Var")

    Examples

    ```
    >>> import numpy as np
    >>> from keysight.pwdatatools import Var
    >>> data = np.array([1, 2, 3])
    >>> vout = Var(data, name='Vout', role='voltage', unit='V')
    >>> new_data = np.array([4.5, 5.5, 6.5]) # new array is float
    >>> vout2 = vout.replace(data=new_data, role='voltage.dc')
    >>> vout
    Var(
        <Float64 data with shape (3,)>,
        name='Vout',
        dims=<empty Dims>,
        role='voltage.dc',
        unit='V',
        attrs={},
    )
    ```

    See also

    [`Var.copy_metadata_in_place`](keysight.pwdatatools.Var.copy_metadata_in_place.md#keysight.pwdatatools.Var.copy_metadata_in_place "keysight.pwdatatools.Var.copy_metadata_in_place")


---

<!-- === 来源: api_reference/main/var/_autosummary/keysight.pwdatatools.Var.role.md === -->

# keysight.pwdatatools.Var.role[](#keysight-pwdatatools-var-role "Link to this heading")

*property* Var.role*: str*[](#keysight.pwdatatools.Var.role "Link to this definition")
:   Metadata defining the function or part the variable serves within a dataset.

    It cannot be any arbitrary string; it must be one of the known,
    valid roles as defined in
    `keysight.pwdatatools.roles.ALL_ROLES`. Examples of valid
    roles are ‘frequency’ and ‘gamma.load’.

    Examples

    Initialize a Var without a role results in the Var having a role
    equal to an empty str.

    ```
    >>> from keysight.pwdatatools import Var
    >>> gain = Var([10, 12, 13], name='Gain')
    >>> gain.role
    ''
    ```

    Initialize a Var with a role.

    ```
    >>> gain = Var([10, 12, 13], name='Gain', role='gain')
    >>> gain.role
    'gain'
    ```

    Set the value of role. Note also how the 2 auxiliary roles
    (power and compression) are sorted when stored in the Var.

    ```
    >>> gain.role = 'gain.power.compression'
    >>> gain.role
    'gain.compression.power'
    ```

    Initialize a Var with an invalid role.

    ```
    >>> gain = Var([10, 12, 13], name='Gain', role='foo')
    <ValueError: Invalid role: 'foo'>
    ```


---

<!-- === 来源: api_reference/main/var/_autosummary/keysight.pwdatatools.Var.select.md === -->

# keysight.pwdatatools.Var.select[](#keysight-pwdatatools-var-select "Link to this heading")

Var.select(*indexer\_dict: IndexerDict | None = None*, */*, *\**, *method: Literal[None, 'pad', 'ffill', 'backfill', 'bfill', 'nearest'] = None*, *limit: int | None = None*, *tolerance: Scalar | ArrayLike | NDArray | pd.Series | None = None*, *\*\*indexer\_kwargs*) → Self[](#keysight.pwdatatools.Var.select "Link to this definition")
:   Return new Var, created by indexing into this Var’s dims.

    This uses pandas indexing under the hood and supports any types of
    indexers that pandas label-based indexing supports. The selection is
    applied to the data and the dims.

    Parameters:
    :   * **indexer\_dict** (*dict of str to Indexer, optional*) – A dict with keys equal to the dimnames and values of scalar
          labels or collection of labels (list, ndarray, Series, Index).
        * **method** (*{‘pad’, ‘ffill’, ‘backfill’, ‘bfill’, ‘nearest’}, optional*) – The method to use for filling holes in reindexed data. If `None`,
          use exact matches only.
        * **limit** (*int, optional*) – The number of consecutive elements to forward- or back-fill.
        * **tolerance** (*scalar or array-like, optional*) – The maximum distance between the original index and the new
          index when `method` is not `None`. The values of the
          index at the matching locations most satisfy the equation
          `abs(index[indexer] - target) <= tolerance`.
        * **indexer\_kwargs, optional** – The keyword arguments form of indexer\_dict. Either
          indexer\_dict or indexer\_kwargs must be provided, but not
          both.

    Returns:
    :   A new Var with the same name, attrs, role, and unit as this Var,
        but with a subset of the data and dims.

    Return type:
    :   [*Var*](../index.md#keysight.pwdatatools.Var "keysight.pwdatatools.Var")

    Raises:
    :   * **ValueError** –
          + If indexer\_dict and indexer\_kwargs are both provided.
            - If neither indexer\_dict nor indexer\_kwargs are provided.
            - If the selector contains invalid values.
        * **TypeError** – If indexer\_dict is not a dict.

    Examples

    The [`Var.select()`](#keysight.pwdatatools.Var.select "keysight.pwdatatools.Var.select") method is a powerful way to index into
    multi-dimensional Vars. It allows indexing based upon arbitrary
    strings and numbers, rather than 0-based or 1-based integer
    positions. Let’s create a new Var to illustrate. We also need to
    create an instance of [`Dims`](../../../metadata/dims/index.md#keysight.pwdatatools.Dims "keysight.pwdatatools.Dims") to store metadata about the
    higher dimensions.

    ```
    >>> import numpy as np
    >>> from keysight.pwdatatools import Dims, Var
    >>> data = np.arange(160).reshape(10, 4, 4)
    >>> dims = Dims(
    ...    ndim=3,
    ...    i_nums=[1, 2, 3, 4],
    ...    i_names=['P1', 'P2', 'P3', 'P4'],
    ...    j_nums=[1, 2, 3, 4],
    ...    j_names=['P1', 'P2', 'P3', 'P4']
    ... )
    >>> s = Var(data, name='S', dims=dims)
    >>> s
    Var(
        <Int32 data with shape (10, 4, 4)>,
        name='S',
        dims=<Dims with nums and names>,
        role='',
        unit=None,
        attrs={},
    )
    ```

    Let’s use the [`Var.select()`](#keysight.pwdatatools.Var.select "keysight.pwdatatools.Var.select") method to get S(2,1) from the
    S-parameters variable. Note the new shape of the data and the new dims.

    ```
    >>> s21 = s.select(i=2, j=1)
    >>> s21
    Var(
        <Int32 data with shape (10, 1, 1)>,
        name='S',
        dims=<Dims with nums and names>,
        role='',
        unit=None,
        attrs={},
    )
    >>> s21.dims
    Dims(
        ndim=3,
        i_nums=[2],
        i_names=['P2'],
        j_nums=[1],
        j_names=['P1'],
    )
    ```

    Let’s do the same thing, except let’s use the portnames this time.

    ```
    >>> s21 = s.select(i='P2', j='P1')
    >>> s21
    Var(
        <Int32 data with shape (10, 1, 1)>,
        name='S',
        dims=<Dims with nums and names>,
        role='',
        unit=None,
        attrs={},
    )
    >>> s21.dims
    Dims(
        ndim=3,
        i_nums=[2],
        i_names=['P2'],
        j_nums=[1],
        j_names=['P1'],
    )
    ```

    Now, let’s use the [`Var.select()`](#keysight.pwdatatools.Var.select "keysight.pwdatatools.Var.select") method to get all the
    S-parameters associated with ports 3 and 4. Just for
    illustration purposes, this time we use a dict instead of
    keyword arguments.

    ```
    >>> s_p3_p4 = s.select({'i': [3, 4], 'j': [3, 4]})
    >>> s_p3_p4
    Var(
        <Int32 data with shape (10, 2, 2)>,
        name='S',
        dims=<Dims with nums and names>,
        role='',
        unit=None,
        attrs={},
    )
    >>> s_p3_p4.dims
    Dims(
        ndim=3,
        i_nums=[3, 4],
        i_names=['P3', 'P4'],
        j_nums=[3, 4],
        j_names=['P3', 'P4'],
    )
    ```


---

<!-- === 来源: api_reference/main/var/_autosummary/keysight.pwdatatools.Var.to_pandas_dataframe.md === -->

# keysight.pwdatatools.Var.to\_pandas\_dataframe[](#keysight-pwdatatools-var-to-pandas-dataframe "Link to this heading")

Var.to\_pandas\_dataframe(*\**, *dtype: DtypeLike = None*, *cols\_nlevels: int = 0*, *cols\_level\_names: bool = True*, *cols\_delim: Tuple[str, str, str] = ('[', ',', ']')*, *multidim\_pref: Literal['dims', 'default\_ints'] = 'dims'*, *dims\_pref: Literal[None, 'nums', 'names'] = None*, *default\_ints: Literal[1, 0] = 1*) → pd.DataFrame[](#keysight.pwdatatools.Var.to_pandas_dataframe "Link to this definition")
:   Make a pandas DataFrame from this Var.

    Parameters:
    :   * **dtype** (*dtype-like, optional*) – The dtype of the returned DataFrame. If `None`, the dtype is
          inferred from the dtype of the data and whether it has invalid
          data points.
        * **cols\_nlevels** (*int, default 0*) – The number of levels to use for MultiIndex columns. If `-1`,
          use the minimum number of levels needed to represent the dims
          of the Var. If `0`, use a flat Index for the cols, embedding
          dimscales, if present. If `1`, use a MultiIndex with 1 level.
          If the value is greater than ndim + 1, fill the extra levels
          with empty strings.
        * **cols\_level\_names** (*bool, default True*) – If `True` set the names of the levels in the returned
          MultiIndex to `'varname'`, `'i'`, `'j'`, etc. Ignored if
          the returned columns Index is flat.
        * **cols\_delim** (*3-tuple of str, default (‘[’, ‘,’, ‘]’)*) – A tuple of str characters for embedding dimension scales into
          the labels of a flat string columns index. The first character
          is the opening delimiter, the second is the delimiter between
          dimension scales, and the third is the closing delimiter. For
          example, the default delimiters produce columns like
          `'PortZ[1]'`, `'S[1,1]'`, `'S[1,2]'`, etc.
        * **multidim\_pref** (*{‘dims’, ‘default\_ints’}, default ‘dims’*) – If `'dims'`, use the Var’s dimscales set by dims\_pref. If
          `'default_ints'`, use the integers set by the default\_ints
          parameter.
        * **dims\_pref** (*{None, ‘nums’, ‘names’}, optional*) – The type of dimension scales preferred for the DataFrame
          columns if the Var’s dims are used. If `'nums'`, use the
          numeric dimscales. If `'names'`, use the string dimscales.
          If `None` (the default), prefer numeric dimscales if they
          exist, otherwise use string dimscales. In all cases, if the
          dimscales are not present, use the integers set by
          default\_ints.
        * **default\_ints** (*{1, 0}, default 1*) – The integer-based indexing to use for the DataFrame columns in
          the event that the Var’s dims are not used. If `1`, use
          1-based ints. If `0`, use 0-based ints.

    Return type:
    :   pandas.DataFrame

    Notes

    Some metadata is not included in the DataFrame, such as unit
    and attrs.

    Examples

    Let’s illustrate with a 3D Var.

    ```
    >>> import numpy as np
    >>> from keysight.pwdatatools import Dims, Var
    >>> data = np.arange(24).reshape(6, 2, 2)
    >>> dims = Dims(
    ...     ndim=3,
    ...     i_names=['P1', 'P2'],
    ...     j_names=['P1', 'P2'],
    ... )
    >>> s = Var(data, name='S', dims=dims)
    ```

    If we use all the default settings, the returned DataFrame uses the
    Var’s dims for columns. The columns index is flat (not a MultiIndex)
    and its labels have embedded dimension scales with the default
    delimiter characters.

    ```
    >>> s.to_pandas_dataframe()
       S['P1','P1']  S['P1','P2']  S['P2','P1']  S['P2','P2']
    0             0             1             2             3
    1             4             5             6             7
    2             8             9            10            11
    3            12            13            14            15
    4            16            17            18            19
    5            20            21            22            23
    ```

    Let’s change the settings to use a MultiIndex for the columns.
    If we set `cols_nlevels=-1`, the number of levels is determined
    by the number of dims of the Var.

    ```
    >>> s.to_pandas_dataframe(cols_nlevels=-1)
    varname   S
    i        P1      P2
    j        P1  P2  P1  P2
    0         0   1   2   3
    1         4   5   6   7
    2         8   9  10  11
    3        12  13  14  15
    4        16  17  18  19
    5        20  21  22  23
    ```

    Let’s turn off the column level names.

    ```
    >>> s.to_pandas_dataframe(cols_nlevels=-1, cols_level_names=False)
         S
        P1      P2
        P1  P2  P1  P2
    0    0   1   2   3
    1    4   5   6   7
    2    8   9  10  11
    3   12  13  14  15
    4   16  17  18  19
    5   20  21  22  23
    ```

    Let’s use integers instead of the Var’s dimscales (using a flat
    columns index this time).

    ```
    >>> s.to_pandas_dataframe(multidim_pref='default_ints')
       S[1,1]  S[1,2]  S[2,1]  S[2,2]
    0       0       1       2       3
    1       4       5       6       7
    2       8       9      10      11
    3      12      13      14      15
    4      16      17      18      19
    5      20      21      22      23
    ```

    Now let’s change the delimiters for the flat columns index.

    ```
    >>> new_delims = ('(', ',', ')')  # must be a 3-tuple of str
    >>> s.to_pandas_dataframe(
    ...     multidim_pref='default_ints', cols_delim=new_delims
    ... )
       S(1,1)  S(1,2)  S(2,1)  S(2,2)
    0       0       1       2       3
    1       4       5       6       7
    2       8       9      10      11
    3      12      13      14      15
    4      16      17      18      19
    5      20      21      22      23
    ```


---

<!-- === 来源: api_reference/main/var/_autosummary/keysight.pwdatatools.Var.to_pandas_series.md === -->

# keysight.pwdatatools.Var.to\_pandas\_series[](#keysight-pwdatatools-var-to-pandas-series "Link to this heading")

Var.to\_pandas\_series(*\**, *dtype: DtypeLike = None*, *name\_nlevels: int = 0*) → pd.Series[](#keysight.pwdatatools.Var.to_pandas_series "Link to this definition")
:   Make a pandas Series from this Var.

    Some of the Var’s metadata is not included in the Series, such as
    unit and attrs. A default pandas integer RangeIndex is always
    used for the index of the Series.

    Parameters:
    :   * **dtype** (*dtype-like, optional*) – The dtype of the returned Series. If `None`, the dtype is
          inferred from the dtype of the data array and whether the
          the Var has invalid data points.
        * **name\_nlevels** (*int, default 0*) – Provided for compatiblity with the `cols_nlevels` parameter
          of the [`to_pandas_dataframe()`](keysight.pwdatatools.Var.to_pandas_dataframe.md#keysight.pwdatatools.Var.to_pandas_dataframe "keysight.pwdatatools.Var.to_pandas_dataframe") method. This parameter
          allows the returned Series to be easily concatenated with a
          DataFrame that has MultiIndex columns. If this parameter is set
          to anything other than `0`, the Series name will be a tuple
          with a length equal to nlevels, filled with empty strings if
          necessary. If `0`, the Series name is a str, not a tuple.

    Return type:
    :   pandas.Series

    Examples

    ```
    >>> from keysight.pwdatatools import Var
    >>> x = Var([1.0, 1.5, 2.0], name='x')
    >>> x.to_pandas_series() # using all defaults
    0    1.0
    1    1.5
    2    2.0
    Name: x, dtype: float64
    ```

    Setting `name_nlevels=3` creates a tuple of length 3 for the
    Series name. This makes it easier to later combine the Series
    with a DataFrame that has MultiIndex columns.

    ```
    >>> x.to_pandas_series(name_nlevels=3)
    0    1.0
    1    1.5
    2    2.0
    Name: (x, , ), dtype: float64
    ```


---

<!-- === 来源: api_reference/main/var/_autosummary/keysight.pwdatatools.Var.unit.md === -->

:   An optional str representing the unit of the variable.

<!-- === 来源: api_reference/main/var/index.md === -->

# Var[](#var "Link to this heading")

This page documents public attributes and methods available in the Var class. For more information and examples, see [Use the Var Class](../../../howto/use_var_class.md#use-var-class).

*class* Var(*data: ArrayLike*, *dtype: [DataType](../../public_submodules/datatypes/_autosummary/keysight.pwdatatools.datatypes.DataType.md#keysight.pwdatatools.datatypes.DataType "keysight.pwdatatools.datatypes.DataType") | Type[[DataType](../../public_submodules/datatypes/_autosummary/keysight.pwdatatools.datatypes.DataType.md#keysight.pwdatatools.datatypes.DataType "keysight.pwdatatools.datatypes.DataType")] | None = None*, *\**, *name: str | None = None*, *dims: DimsLike | None = None*, *role: str | None = None*, *unit: str | None = None*, *attrs: AttrsDictLike | None = None*)[](#keysight.pwdatatools.Var "Link to this definition")
:   An object that holds data and metadata for a variable.

    Parameters:
    :   * **data** (*ArrayLike*) – An array-like object containing the data values.
        * **dtype** (*DataType, optional*) – The datatype. If not specified, it is inferred from the data.
        * **name** (*str, optional*) – A str name for the variable. If not specified, the name is set
          to an empty string.
        * **dims** (*DimsLike, optional*) – Optional metadata relating to the variable’s dimensions.
        * **role** (*str, optional*) – Metadata defining the part or function this variable serves
          within a dataset (for example, ‘frequency’ or ‘gamma.load’).
        * **unit** (*str, optional*) – An optional string representing the unit of the variable.
        * **attrs** (*dict-like, optional*) – An optional dict or dict-like object with arbitrary attributes
          (metadata) for the variable. The keys must be str and the values
          must be str, int, float, complex, bool, None, dict, list, or
          numpy.ndarray. Nested dicts and lists are allowed.

    Examples

    Below we create a 1D Var. The data is required, but all other
    parameters are optional.

    ```
    >>> import numpy as np
    >>> from keysight.pwdatatools import Var
    >>> data = np.array([1, 2, 3])
    >>> vout = Var(data, name='Vout', role='voltage', unit='V')
    >>> vout.info()
    kind           -
    role     voltage
    dtype      Int32
    shape       (3,)
    dims           -
    unit           V
    min            1
    max            3
    null           -
    nan            -
    attrs          -
    Name: Vout, dtype: string
    ```

    Now, let’s instantiate a 1D Var with a numpy MaskedArray to represent
    missing or invalid data points. Note that the masked value(s) become
    the null value(s) and are not included in the min and max values.

    ```
    >>> ma = np.ma.masked_array([3, 4], mask=[True, False])
    >>> v_masked = Var(ma, name='masked')
    >>> v_masked.info()
    kind         -
    role         -
    dtype    Int32
    shape     (2,)
    dims         -
    unit         -
    min          4
    max          4
    null         1
    nan          -
    attrs        -
    Name: masked, dtype: string
    ```

    Next, let’s create a 2D Var.

    ```
    >>> data = np.random.random(6).reshape((3, 2))
    >>> portz = Var(data, name='PortZ', role='impedance.port', unit='Ohm')
    ```

    Using pandas or polars can be very complementary to pwdatatools. Here,
    let’s create a pandas DataFrame in order to more easily visualize our
    2D Var. Note that 1-based integer indexes are used for the higher
    dimension (the numbers inside of the square brackets).

    Note

    There are other options for indexing into the higher dimensions
    of a variable, such as 0-based integer and string name indexes.

    ```
    >>> df = portz.to_pandas_dataframe()
    >>> print(df)
       PortZ[1]  PortZ[2]
    0  0.551544  0.467092
    1  0.813803  0.629148
    2  0.146644  0.529128
    ```

    Or, if you prefer, you can use MultiIndex columns to get rid of the delimiters.

    ```
    >>> df = portz.to_pandas_dataframe(cols_nlevels=2)
    >>> print(df)
    varname     PortZ
    i               1         2
    0        0.551544  0.467092
    1        0.813803  0.629148
    2        0.146644  0.529128
    ```

    See also

    For more information on pandas DataFrame indexing, see
    [pandas DataFrame Indexing](../../../core_concepts/pandas_dataframe_indexing.md#pandas-dataframe-indexing) and also the pandas documentation.

    Finally, let’s create a 3D Var. This time, we have two higher
    dimensions (always named “i” and “j”). Let’s define string names to
    use as indexes along these higher dimensions instead of integers.

    ```
    >>> data = np.random.random(20).reshape((5, 2, 2))
    >>> dims = Dims(ndim=3, i_names=['P1', 'P2'], j_names=['P1', 'P2'])
    >>> s = Var(data, name='S', dims=dims, role='network_parameters.s')
    >>> df = s.to_pandas_dataframe(cols_nlevels=3)
    >>> print(df)
    varname         S
    i              P1                  P2
    j              P1        P2        P1        P2
    0        0.551284  0.070031  0.586010  0.885456
    1        0.626966  0.234664  0.932699  0.882455
    2        0.137112  0.213657  0.657413  0.388838
    3        0.410304  0.545891  0.158400  0.034383
    4        0.009548  0.688676  0.918804  0.848939
    ```

# keysight.pwdatatools.AttrsDict.update[](#keysight-pwdatatools-attrsdict-update "Link to this heading")

AttrsDict.update([*E*, ]*\*\*F*) → None.  Update D from mapping/iterable E and F.[](#keysight.pwdatatools.AttrsDict.update "Link to this definition")
:   If E present and has a .keys() method, does: for k in E: D[k] = E[k]
    If E present and lacks .keys() method, does: for (k, v) in E: D[k] = v
    In either case, this is followed by: for k, v in F.items(): D[k] = v

# AttrsDict[](#attrsdict "Link to this heading")

This page gives an overview of public attributes and methods available for the AttrsDict class. This class is a type-restricted dict that stores arbitrary attributes (metadata) for the [`Var`](../../main/var/index.md#keysight.pwdatatools.Var "keysight.pwdatatools.Var"), [`Block`](../../main/block/index.md#keysight.pwdatatools.Block "keysight.pwdatatools.Block"), and [`Group`](../../main/group/index.md#keysight.pwdatatools.Group "keysight.pwdatatools.Group") classes. For more information and examples, see the [Use the Var Class](../../../howto/use_var_class.md#use-var-class), [Use the Block Class](../../../howto/use_block_class.md#use-block-class) and [Use the Group Class](../../../howto/use_group_class.md#use-group-class).

*class* AttrsDict(*mapping: PropertyDictLike | None = None*, *\*\*kwargs*)[](#keysight.pwdatatools.AttrsDict "Link to this definition")
:   A mutable dict-like object to store arbitrary attributes.

    This object is used to store the attributes for all Var, Block,
    and Group objects.

    All keys must be str, and all values must be bool, complex, float,
    int, str, None, numpy ndarray, or an instance of
    `CustomProperty`.

| --- | --- |
| [`AttrsDict.key_type`](_autosummary/keysight.pwdatatools.AttrsDict.key_type.md#keysight.pwdatatools.AttrsDict.key_type "keysight.pwdatatools.AttrsDict.key_type") | Return type of keys. |
| [`AttrsDict.reserved_keys`](_autosummary/keysight.pwdatatools.AttrsDict.reserved_keys.md#keysight.pwdatatools.AttrsDict.reserved_keys "keysight.pwdatatools.AttrsDict.reserved_keys") | Return frozenset of reserved keys. |

<!-- === 来源: api_reference/metadata/dims/_autosummary/keysight.pwdatatools.Dims.__init__.md === -->

# keysight.pwdatatools.Dims.\_\_init\_\_[](#keysight-pwdatatools-dims-init "Link to this heading")

Dims.\_\_init\_\_(*\**, *ndim: int*, *i\_nums: ArrayLike | None = None*, *i\_names: ArrayLike | None = None*, *j\_nums: ArrayLike | None = None*, *j\_names: ArrayLike | None = None*)[](#keysight.pwdatatools.Dims.__init__ "Link to this definition")
:   Initialize the Dims object.

    Parameters:
    :   * **ndim** (*int*) – The number of dimensions of Var. Note that this number includes
          the shared dimension.
        * **i\_nums** (*numeric array-like, optional*) – The numeric scale for the “i” dimension.
        * **i\_names** (*string array-like, optional*) – The string scale for the “i” dimension.
        * **j\_nums** (*numeric array-like, optional*) – The numeric scale for the “j” dimension.
        * **j\_names** (*string array-like, optional*) – The string scale for the “j” dimension.

    Examples

    A 1D Var always has empty Dims because there are no higher
    dimensions for a 1D Var, and [`Dims`](../index.md#keysight.pwdatatools.Dims "keysight.pwdatatools.Dims") only stores metadata
    for higher dimensions.

    ```
    >>> from keysight.pwdatatools import Var
    >>> x = Var([10, 12, 13], name='x')
    >>> x.dims
    Dims(ndim=1)
    >>> x.has_empty_dims()
    True
    ```

    Initializing a 2D Var or 3D Var without explicitly providing a
    dims argument also results in empty Dims, just like the 1D case.

    ```
    >>> import numpy as np
    >>> data_2d = np.random.random(6).reshape((3, 2))
    >>> var_2d = Var(data_2d, name='var_2d')
    >>> var_2d.dims
    Dims(ndim=2)
    >>> var_2d.has_empty_dims()
    True
    >>> data_3d = np.random.random(20).reshape((5, 2, 2))
    >>> var_3d = Var(data_3d, name='var_3d')
    >>> var_3d.dims
    Dims(ndim=3)
    >>> var_3d.has_empty_dims()
    True
    ```

    Now, let’s initialize a 2D Var with dims that include i\_names,
    which enables indexing along the “i” dimension using strings
    instead of integers.

    ```
    >>> from keysight.pwdatatools import Dims
    >>> import numpy as np
    >>> dims = Dims(ndim=2, i_names=['P1', 'P2'])
    >>> var_2d_dims = Var(data_2d, name='var_2d_dims', dims=dims)
    >>> var_2d_dims.dims
    Dims(
        ndim=2,
        i_nums=None,
        i_names=['P1', 'P2'],
    )
    ```

    Below, we initialize a 3D Var with dims that include i\_nums,
    which enables indexing along the “i” and “j” dimensions using
    arbitrary integers, rather than just 0-based or 1-based integers.

    ```
    >>> dims = Dims(ndim=3, i_nums=[3, 4], j_nums=[7, 8])
    >>> var_3d_dims = Var(data_3d, name='s', dims=dims)
    >>> var_3d_dims.dims
    Dims(
        ndim=3,
        i_nums=[3, 4],
        i_names=None,
        j_nums=[7, 8],
        j_names=None,
    )
    ```

    Notes

    An instance of [`Dims`](../index.md#keysight.pwdatatools.Dims "keysight.pwdatatools.Dims") is stored in the [`Var.dims`](../../../main/var/_autosummary/keysight.pwdatatools.Var.dims.md#keysight.pwdatatools.Var.dims "keysight.pwdatatools.Var.dims")
    property. Since [`Dims`](../index.md#keysight.pwdatatools.Dims "keysight.pwdatatools.Dims") only stores metadata related to higher
    dimensions, it is only useful for multi-dimensional Vars. However,
    even 1D Vars have an instance of [`Dims`](../index.md#keysight.pwdatatools.Dims "keysight.pwdatatools.Dims") stored in the
    [`Var.dims`](../../../main/var/_autosummary/keysight.pwdatatools.Var.dims.md#keysight.pwdatatools.Var.dims "keysight.pwdatatools.Var.dims") property; but, a 1D Var’s dims are always empty.

    The [`Dims`](../index.md#keysight.pwdatatools.Dims "keysight.pwdatatools.Dims") object is used to store metadata for the “i” and “j”
    dimensions of a Var. The “i” dimension is the first higher-order
    dimension, and the “j” dimension is the second higher-order dimension.
    Note that a Var’s data also has a “shared” dimension, which is axis 0
    in a numpy ndarray or pandas DataFrame. Metadata for the shared
    dimension cannot be stored in this object.

    The highest dimensionality supported is a matrix Var, which has two
    higher-order dimensions (“i” and “j”), plus a shared dimension
    (axis 0). Therefore, this object only supports storing metadata for
    vector Vars (which have an “i” dimension) and matrix Vars (which have
    both “i” and “j” dimensions).

    For each dimension, it is possible to store two scales (at most):
    “nums” and “names”. The “nums” scale stores numeric values
    (usually integers, but floats and complex numbers are supported).
    The “names” scale stores string values.

# keysight.pwdatatools.Dims.is\_empty[](#keysight-pwdatatools-dims-is-empty "Link to this heading")

Dims.is\_empty() → bool[](#keysight.pwdatatools.Dims.is_empty "Link to this definition")
:   Return True if None of the dimensions have associated nums or names scales.

    If the number of dimensions is 1, the Dims object is always empty.
    If the number of dimensions is 2 or 3, the Dims object is empty if
    neither the “i” nor the “j” dimension have associated scales.

    Returns:
    :   True if the Dims object is empty.

    Return type:
    :   *bool*

# keysight.pwdatatools.Dims.replace[](#keysight-pwdatatools-dims-replace "Link to this heading")

Dims.replace(*\*\*kwargs: Any*) → [Dims](../index.md#keysight.pwdatatools.Dims "keysight.pwdatatools._core.variables.dims.Dims")[](#keysight.pwdatatools.Dims.replace "Link to this definition")

# Dims[](#dims "Link to this heading")

This page gives an overview of public attributes and methods available for the Dims class. This object stores metadata related to the higher dimensions of a `Var` and can be used for indexing into multi-dimensional Vars. For more information and examples, see [Use the Var Class](../../../howto/use_var_class.md#use-var-class).

*class* Dims(*\**, *ndim: int*, *i\_nums: ArrayLike | None = None*, *i\_names: ArrayLike | None = None*, *j\_nums: ArrayLike | None = None*, *j\_names: ArrayLike | None = None*)[](#keysight.pwdatatools.Dims "Link to this definition")
:   An object to store metadata related to dimensions for a [`Var`](../../main/var/index.md#keysight.pwdatatools.Var "keysight.pwdatatools.Var").

    Parameters:
    :   * **ndim** (*int*) – The number of dimensions of Var. Note that this number includes
          the shared dimension.
        * **i\_nums** (*numeric array-like, optional*) – The numeric scale for the “i” dimension.
        * **i\_names** (*string array-like, optional*) – The string scale for the “i” dimension.
        * **j\_nums** (*numeric array-like, optional*) – The numeric scale for the “j” dimension.
        * **j\_names** (*string array-like, optional*) – The string scale for the “j” dimension.

    Examples

    A 1D Var always has empty Dims because there are no higher
    dimensions for a 1D Var, and [`Dims`](#keysight.pwdatatools.Dims "keysight.pwdatatools.Dims") only stores metadata
    for higher dimensions.

    ```
    >>> from keysight.pwdatatools import Var
    >>> x = Var([10, 12, 13], name='x')
    >>> x.dims
    Dims(ndim=1)
    >>> x.has_empty_dims()
    True
    ```

    Initializing a 2D Var or 3D Var without explicitly providing a
    dims argument also results in empty Dims, just like the 1D case.

    ```
    >>> import numpy as np
    >>> data_2d = np.random.random(6).reshape((3, 2))
    >>> var_2d = Var(data_2d, name='var_2d')
    >>> var_2d.dims
    Dims(ndim=2)
    >>> var_2d.has_empty_dims()
    True
    >>> data_3d = np.random.random(20).reshape((5, 2, 2))
    >>> var_3d = Var(data_3d, name='var_3d')
    >>> var_3d.dims
    Dims(ndim=3)
    >>> var_3d.has_empty_dims()
    True
    ```

    Now, let’s initialize a 2D Var with dims that include i\_names,
    which enables indexing along the “i” dimension using strings
    instead of integers.

    ```
    >>> from keysight.pwdatatools import Dims
    >>> import numpy as np
    >>> dims = Dims(ndim=2, i_names=['P1', 'P2'])
    >>> var_2d_dims = Var(data_2d, name='var_2d_dims', dims=dims)
    >>> var_2d_dims.dims
    Dims(
        ndim=2,
        i_nums=None,
        i_names=['P1', 'P2'],
    )
    ```

    Below, we initialize a 3D Var with dims that include i\_nums,
    which enables indexing along the “i” and “j” dimensions using
    arbitrary integers, rather than just 0-based or 1-based integers.

    ```
    >>> dims = Dims(ndim=3, i_nums=[3, 4], j_nums=[7, 8])
    >>> var_3d_dims = Var(data_3d, name='s', dims=dims)
    >>> var_3d_dims.dims
    Dims(
        ndim=3,
        i_nums=[3, 4],
        i_names=None,
        j_nums=[7, 8],
        j_names=None,
    )
    ```

    Notes

    An instance of [`Dims`](#keysight.pwdatatools.Dims "keysight.pwdatatools.Dims") is stored in the [`Var.dims`](../../main/var/_autosummary/keysight.pwdatatools.Var.dims.md#keysight.pwdatatools.Var.dims "keysight.pwdatatools.Var.dims")
    property. Since [`Dims`](#keysight.pwdatatools.Dims "keysight.pwdatatools.Dims") only stores metadata related to higher
    dimensions, it is only useful for multi-dimensional Vars. However,
    even 1D Vars have an instance of [`Dims`](#keysight.pwdatatools.Dims "keysight.pwdatatools.Dims") stored in the
    [`Var.dims`](../../main/var/_autosummary/keysight.pwdatatools.Var.dims.md#keysight.pwdatatools.Var.dims "keysight.pwdatatools.Var.dims") property; but, a 1D Var’s dims are always empty.

    The [`Dims`](#keysight.pwdatatools.Dims "keysight.pwdatatools.Dims") object is used to store metadata for the “i” and “j”
    dimensions of a Var. The “i” dimension is the first higher-order
    dimension, and the “j” dimension is the second higher-order dimension.
    Note that a Var’s data also has a “shared” dimension, which is axis 0
    in a numpy ndarray or pandas DataFrame. Metadata for the shared
    dimension cannot be stored in this object.

    The highest dimensionality supported is a matrix Var, which has two
    higher-order dimensions (“i” and “j”), plus a shared dimension
    (axis 0). Therefore, this object only supports storing metadata for
    vector Vars (which have an “i” dimension) and matrix Vars (which have
    both “i” and “j” dimensions).

    For each dimension, it is possible to store two scales (at most):
    “nums” and “names”. The “nums” scale stores numeric values
    (usually integers, but floats and complex numbers are supported).
    The “names” scale stores string values.

    See also

    [`Var.select()`](../../main/var/_autosummary/keysight.pwdatatools.Var.select.md#keysight.pwdatatools.Var.select "keysight.pwdatatools.Var.select")
    :   Uses the Dims object to select data along the “i” and “j” dimensions.

    [`Var.dims`](../../main/var/_autosummary/keysight.pwdatatools.Var.dims.md#keysight.pwdatatools.Var.dims "keysight.pwdatatools.Var.dims")
    :   The Dims object is stored in the Var object’s dims property.

    [`Var.has_empty_dims()`](../../main/var/_autosummary/keysight.pwdatatools.Var.has_empty_dims.md#keysight.pwdatatools.Var.has_empty_dims "keysight.pwdatatools.Var.has_empty_dims")
    :   Check if a Var has empty Dims.

**Attributes**

|  |  |
| --- | --- |
| [`Dims.ndim`](_autosummary/keysight.pwdatatools.Dims.ndim.md#keysight.pwdatatools.Dims.ndim "keysight.pwdatatools.Dims.ndim") | Return the number of dimensions. |

**Magic Methods**

|  |  |
| --- | --- |
| [`Dims.__bool__`](_autosummary/keysight.pwdatatools.Dims.__bool__.md#keysight.pwdatatools.Dims.__bool__ "keysight.pwdatatools.Dims.__bool__")() | Return True if the Dims object is not empty. |
| [`Dims.__eq__`](_autosummary/keysight.pwdatatools.Dims.__eq__.md#keysight.pwdatatools.Dims.__eq__ "keysight.pwdatatools.Dims.__eq__")(other) | Return True if the Dims object is equal to another object. |
| [`Dims.__init__`](_autosummary/keysight.pwdatatools.Dims.__init__.md#keysight.pwdatatools.Dims.__init__ "keysight.pwdatatools.Dims.__init__")(\*, ndim[, i\_nums, i\_names, ...]) | Initialize the Dims object. |
| [`Dims.__repr__`](_autosummary/keysight.pwdatatools.Dims.__repr__.md#keysight.pwdatatools.Dims.__repr__ "keysight.pwdatatools.Dims.__repr__")() | Return a string representation of the Dims object. |
| [`Dims.__repr_short__`](_autosummary/keysight.pwdatatools.Dims.__repr_short__.md#keysight.pwdatatools.Dims.__repr_short__ "keysight.pwdatatools.Dims.__repr_short__")() | Return a short string representation of the Dims object. |

| [`Dims.is_empty`](_autosummary/keysight.pwdatatools.Dims.is_empty.md#keysight.pwdatatools.Dims.is_empty "keysight.pwdatatools.Dims.is_empty")() | Return True if None of the dimensions have associated nums or names scales. |

# keysight.pwdatatools.calc.db\_to\_power[](#keysight-pwdatatools-calc-db-to-power "Link to this heading")

db\_to\_power(*db: NumericScalar*, */*, *ref=1*) → NumericPythonScalar[](#keysight.pwdatatools.calc.db_to_power "Link to this definition")

db\_to\_power(*db: NumericNDArrayLike*, */*, *ref=1*) → NumericNDArray
:   Convert decibels to linear power value or ratio.

    The power is calculated as ref \* 10\*\*(db / 10). This function is the
    inverse of [`power_to_db()`](keysight.pwdatatools.calc.power_to_db.md#keysight.pwdatatools.calc.power_to_db "keysight.pwdatatools.calc.power_to_db").

    Parameters:
    :   * **db** (*numeric numpy.ndarray, array-like, scalar, or Var*) – The input data in decibels.
        * **ref** (*float or int, default 1*) – Reference value for ratio. Default is 1.

    Returns:
    :   **power** – The linear power of the input.

    Return type:
    :   numeric numpy.ndarray or Python scalar


---

# keysight.pwdatatools.calc.db\_to\_voltage[](#keysight-pwdatatools-calc-db-to-voltage "Link to this heading")

db\_to\_voltage(*db: NumericScalar*, */*, *ref=1*) → NumericPythonScalar[](#keysight.pwdatatools.calc.db_to_voltage "Link to this definition")

db\_to\_voltage(*db: NumericNDArrayLike*, */*, *ref=1*) → NumericNDArray
:   Convert decibels to linear voltage value or ratio.

    The voltage is calculated as ref \* 10\*\*(db / 20). This function is the
    inverse of [`voltage_to_db()`](keysight.pwdatatools.calc.voltage_to_db.md#keysight.pwdatatools.calc.voltage_to_db "keysight.pwdatatools.calc.voltage_to_db").

    Parameters:
    :   * **db** (*numeric numpy.ndarray, array-like, scalar, or Var*) – The input data in decibels.
        * **ref** (*float or int, default 1*) – Reference value for ratio. Default is 1.

    Returns:
    :   **voltage** – The linear voltage of the input.

    Return type:
    :   numeric numpy.ndarray or Python scalar

| [`gamma_to_z`](_autosummary/keysight.pwdatatools.calc.gamma_to_z.md#keysight.pwdatatools.calc.gamma_to_z "keysight.pwdatatools.calc.gamma_to_z")() | Calculate impedance from Gamma and a reference impedance. |

# keysight.pwdatatools.datatypes.FillValues[](#keysight-pwdatatools-datatypes-fillvalues "Link to this heading")

*class* FillValues(*boolean: bool = True*, *complexfloating: complex = nan + nanj*, *floating: float = nan*, *integer: int = 999999*, *string: str = 'null'*)[](#keysight.pwdatatools.datatypes.FillValues "Link to this definition")
:   Define fill values for masked data.

    \_\_init\_\_(*boolean: bool = True*, *complexfloating: complex = nan + nanj*, *floating: float = nan*, *integer: int = 999999*, *string: str = 'null'*)[](#keysight.pwdatatools.datatypes.FillValues.__init__ "Link to this definition")
    :   Initialize FillValues instance.

        Parameters:
        :   * **boolean, default True** – Fill value for boolean data.
            * **complexfloating, default NaN + NaN \* 1j** – Fill value for complex data.
            * **floating, default NaN** – Fill value for float data.
            * **integer, default 999999** – Fill value for integer data.
            * **string, default “null”** – Fill value for string data.

    Methods

    |  |  |
    | --- | --- |
    | [`__init__`](#keysight.pwdatatools.datatypes.FillValues.__init__ "keysight.pwdatatools.datatypes.FillValues.__init__")([boolean, complexfloating, ...]) | Initialize FillValues instance. |
    | `get_fill_value`(dtype) | Return fill value for the specified DataType. |
    | `replace`(\*\*kwargs) | Return new instance with any specified values replaced. |

<!-- === 来源: api_reference/public_submodules/datatypes/index.md === -->

# datatypes[](#module-keysight.pwdatatools.datatypes "Link to this heading")

The `keysight.pwdatatools` library defines datatypes that describe
the data stored in the `Var` class. The base datatype class is
[`DataType`](_autosummary/keysight.pwdatatools.datatypes.DataType.md#keysight.pwdatatools.datatypes.DataType "keysight.pwdatatools.datatypes.DataType"), and all other datatypes are subclasses of this class.

The following datatypes are defined:

|  |  |
| --- | --- |
| [`Boolean`](_autosummary/keysight.pwdatatools.datatypes.Boolean.md#keysight.pwdatatools.datatypes.Boolean "keysight.pwdatatools.datatypes.Boolean")() | Boolean data type. |
| [`Complex64`](_autosummary/keysight.pwdatatools.datatypes.Complex64.md#keysight.pwdatatools.datatypes.Complex64 "keysight.pwdatatools.datatypes.Complex64")() | 64-bit complex data type. |
| [`Complex128`](_autosummary/keysight.pwdatatools.datatypes.Complex128.md#keysight.pwdatatools.datatypes.Complex128 "keysight.pwdatatools.datatypes.Complex128")() | 128-bit complex data type. |
| [`DataType`](_autosummary/keysight.pwdatatools.datatypes.DataType.md#keysight.pwdatatools.datatypes.DataType "keysight.pwdatatools.datatypes.DataType")() | Base class for all PathWave Data Tools data types. |
| [`FillValues`](_autosummary/keysight.pwdatatools.datatypes.FillValues.md#keysight.pwdatatools.datatypes.FillValues "keysight.pwdatatools.datatypes.FillValues")([boolean, complexfloating, ...]) | Define fill values for masked data. |
| [`Float32`](_autosummary/keysight.pwdatatools.datatypes.Float32.md#keysight.pwdatatools.datatypes.Float32 "keysight.pwdatatools.datatypes.Float32")() | 32-bit floating point data type. |
| [`Float64`](_autosummary/keysight.pwdatatools.datatypes.Float64.md#keysight.pwdatatools.datatypes.Float64 "keysight.pwdatatools.datatypes.Float64")() | 64-bit floating point data type. |
| [`Int8`](_autosummary/keysight.pwdatatools.datatypes.Int8.md#keysight.pwdatatools.datatypes.Int8 "keysight.pwdatatools.datatypes.Int8")() | 8-bit integer data type. |

In addition to the DataType objects, the `datatypes` module also
defines the [`FillValues`](_autosummary/keysight.pwdatatools.datatypes.FillValues.md#keysight.pwdatatools.datatypes.FillValues "keysight.pwdatatools.datatypes.FillValues") class, which contains scalar values that
can be used to fill null values in Vars.

<!-- === 来源: api_reference/public_submodules/roles/index.md === -->

# roles[](#module-keysight.pwdatatools.roles "Link to this heading")

The `keysight.pwdatatools` library uses a concept of “roles” to
classify variables according to the functions they serve in a dataset.
Roles are used to generate variable names, find variables and blocks in
hierarchical datasets, and to make methods and functions easier to use.

A role is defined by a main role and zero or more auxiliary roles (also
called “aux” roles). The main role defines the general function of the
variable, while the auxiliary role(s) are more specific. For example,
a variable with a main role of “gain” and an aux role of “compression”
would be a variable that represents the gain compression of a device.
A variable with a main role of “gain” and an aux role of “power” would
be a variable that represents the power gain of a device. Because a
role may have more than one aux role, it’s possible to define a role
with a main role of “gain” aux roles of both “compression” and “power”.

Users are *not* allowed to define arbitrary roles; they must be selected
from predefined roles. All valid main roles and auxiliary roles are
stored in the `keysight.pwdatatools.roles.ALL_ROLES` in a frozen
dict. Note that some main roles have no aux roles, while others have
several.

All Roles[](#id1 "Link to this table")

| Main Role | Auxiliary Roles |
| --- | --- |
| angle | effective, phi, theta |
| capacitance | – |
| conductance | – |
| current | alternating, direct, enumerated |
| distortion | ampm |
| directivity | – |
| efficiency | drain, power-added, radiation |
| field | electric, magnetic, phi, theta |
| frequency | enumerated, fundamental, harmonic, load, primary, source |
| gain | compression, power, s-parameters, transducer, vector, voltage |
| gamma | fundamental, harmonic, input, load, output, optimum, source |
| impedance | fundamental, harmonic, input, load, output, port, reference, source |
| index | – |
| inductance | – |
| length | – |
| network\_parameters | g, h, s, t, x, y, z |
| noise\_figure | minimum |
| parameter | – |
| port | name |
| power | available, delivered, fundamental, harmonic, input, load, noise, output, radiated, source, spectral-density |
| resistance | noise |
| temperature | – |
| time | – |
| voltage | ac, dc, enumerated, noise |
| wave | enumerated, fundamental, harmonic, incident, reflected |

## How to create roles[](#how-to-create-roles "Link to this heading")

Roles are Python strings created by combining the main role and aux role(s) with period(s). A main role is always required, but aux role(s) are optional. So, continuing with our previous gain variable example, a variable could have any of the following roles:

* “gain”
* “gain.compression”
* “gain.power”
* “gain.compression.power”

A variable’s role is stored in the `Var.role` attribute as a str. The str value is always automatically finalized and validated before it is stored in the Var. Finalization includes alphabetical sorting of the aux roles and converting everything to lowercase. An exception is raised if the value is not a known, valid role.

```
>>> from keysight import pwdatatools as pwdt
>>> # the first example shows creation of a Var wihtout a role results in an empty str role
>>> v = pwdt.Var([1, 2, 3], name="Vin")
>>> v.role
''
>>> # the next example shows that the aux roles are sorted alphabetically
>>> zrefload = pwdt.Var([50 + 1j, 49 +1.5j], name="ZrefLoad", role='impedance.reference.load', unit="Ohm")
>>> zrefload.role
'impedance.load.reference'
>>> # the next example shows that the main and aux roles are converted to lowercase
>>> gamma_load = pwdt.Var([0.5 + 0.5j, 0.6 + 0.6j], name="GammaLoad", role="Gamma.Load")
>>> gamma_load.role
'gamma.load'
```

We can also add a role to a `Var` after it has been created. This can be achieved in two different ways: 1) directly set the value of the `Var.role` attribute, or 2) invoke the `Var.add_role()` method. One difference between the `Var.add_role()` method and setting the `Var.role` attribute is that the former also checks if a role to be added is a subrole of an existing role. If it is, no changes are made. So, if we try to add a role of “gain” to a `Var` that already has the role of “gain.compression”, the role will not be modified because “gain” is a subrole of “gain.compression”. In other words, “gain.compression” is a superset of “gain”. However, if we try to add a role of “gain.compression” to a `Var` that already has a role of “gain”, the role is indeed updated to “gain.compression”. This is because “gain.compression” is a more specific role than “gain”. The `Var.add_role()` method always keeps the most specific role, whether that is the existing role or the newer role.

```
>>> # start with a Var with no role
>>> v = pwdt.Var([1, 2, 3], name="Vin")
>>> v.role
''
>>> v.role = "voltage" # set the role directly
>>> v.role
'voltage'
>>> v.add_role("voltage.dc") # add a more specific role
>>> v.role
'voltage.dc'
>>> v.add_role("voltage") # adding a less specific role has no effect
>>> v.role
'voltage.dc'
>>> v.role = 'voltage'  # but direct setting with less specific role does change it
>>> v.role
'voltage'
```

## Roles in datafiles[](#roles-in-datafiles "Link to this heading")

If roles are directly stored in a datafile, or if they are able to be inferred, they will be automatically added to the `Var.role` attribute when a datafile is read.

```
>>> from keysight pwdatatools as pwdt
>>> block = pwdt.read_file_as_block("/my_datafile.pwdt", loc="my_block")
>>> block["ZrefLoad"].role
'impedance.load.reference'
```

Some datafile formats are able to store roles as metadata. The most capable file format with respect to metadata storage is the native datafile format for PathWave Data Tools (with a .pwdt file extension). The pwdt format can store all supported variable roles. ADS datasets (.ds file extension) also have some role-storing capabilities, but the variety of supported roles is limited. Some other datafile formats do not store roles, but do store units, and many times, the roles can be inferred from the units. Good examples of datafiles with units are SystemVue datasets (inside of workspace files with a .wsv extension) and ICCAP files (.mdm). Other datafile formats are so narrow and targeted that roles can be inferred from variable names or other context. Examples of these datafile formats are Touchstone, SMatrixIO, S2PMDIF, far field datafiles (from Keysight EM tools and HFSS), and load pull (from Maury and Focus measurement systems). Almost all variables created by reading these datafiles have roles.

Some roles are mapped to default variable names that get used when reading datafiles. For example, if a variable has a role of “power.available.input”, then the variable name will be set to “PinAvail”. The following table shows all roles that set variable names.

Role to default variable name mapping[](#id2 "Link to this table")

| Role | Default Variable Name |
| --- | --- |
| angle.phi | phi |
| angle.theta | theta |
| current.direct.enumerated | I\* |
| current.direct.input | Iin |
| current.direct.output | Iout |
| current.direct.input.quiescent | Iq\_in |
| current.direct.output.quiescent | Iq\_out |
| distortion.ampm | AMPM |
| efficiency.drain | DrainEff |
| efficiency.power-added | PAE |
| field.electric.phi | EPhi |
| field.electric.theta | ETheta |
| frequency.enumerated | F\* |
| frequency.primary | freq |
| gain.power | GainP |
| gain.transducer | GainT |
| gain.s-parameters.transducer | GainT\_s |
| gain.vector | VectorGain |
| gain.transducer.vector | VectorGainT |
| gamma.input | GammaIn |
| gamma.load | GammaLoad |
| gamma.output | GammaOut |
| gamma.source | GammaSource |
| gamma.optimum.source | Sopt |
| impedance.input | Zin |
| impedance.load | ZLoad |
| impedance.source | ZSource |
| impedance.reference | Zref |
| impedance.load.reference | ZrefLoad |
| impedance.port | PortZ |
| impedance.reference.source | ZrefSource |
| port.name | PortName |
| power.available.input | PinAvail |
| power.delivered.input | PinDel |
| power.delivered.load | PLoad |
| power.available.source | PSource |
| network\_parameters.s | S |
| noise\_figure.minimum | NFmin |
| resistance.noise | Rn |
| voltage.dc.enumerated | V\* |
| voltage.dc.input | Vin |
| voltage.dc.output | Vout |
| voltage.dc.input.quiescent | Vq\_in |
| voltage.dc.output.quiescent | Vq\_out |
| wave.enumerated.incident | a\* |
| wave.enumerated.reflected | b\* |

Note

There are some roles that have an “enumerated” auxiliary role and an asterisk in the default variable name. This is means the final variable name will have an integer replacement for the asterisk. For example, a variable with a role of “frequency.enumerated” will have a variable name of “F1”, “F2”, “F3”, etc. The integer replacement typically starts at 1 (although it could start at 0) and increments for each variable with the same role.

The information in the above table is stored as an immutable dict in `keysight.pwdatatools.roles.VARNAMES_DEFAULT`. If you would like to use different variable names or map a new role to varname entry, you can access a mutable global option `keysight.pwdatatools.reading.varnames`, as shown below.

```
>>> from keysight import pwdatatools as pwdt
>>> pwdt.options.reading.varnames["power.available.input"] = "Pin"  # changing already-existing entry
>>> pwdt.options.reading.varnames["noise_figure"] = "NF"  # adding new entry
>>> group = pwdt.read_file_as_group("/my_datafile.pwdt")  # this read operation uses the new varnames
```

## How to use roles[](#how-to-use-roles "Link to this heading")

Roles are used in various ways in the `keysight.pwdatatools` library. They are used extensively by the `LoadPullBlock` class. For example, it looks for variables with roles of “gamma”, “power”, “impedance.load.reference” when the user instantiates a `LoadPullBlock`, and when various methods are invoked, such as `LoadPullBlock.at_gcomp()`, `LoadPullBlock.contourplot()`, and `LoadPullBlock.gamma_ivar()`. In all of these methods (plus many more) it is necessary to know which variable(s) are playing the roles of the swept gamma ivar, swept power ivar, and load impedance reference.

See also

The [Work with Load Pull Data](../../../howto/work_with_loadpull_data.md#work-with-load-pull-data), the [Load Pull Examples](../../../examples/loadpull/index.md#load-pull-examples) and the `LoadPullBlock` class documentation for more information.

Another way to make use of roles is to filter datasets according to variable roles. We can iterate through variables in a `Block` and find variables with any desired role(s). To use this approach we use the `Var.has_role()` method to check if each variable has a particular role. For example, if we want to find all variables with a main role of “power”, we can do the following:

```
>>> block = pwdt.read_file_as_block("/my_datafile.pwdt", loc="my_block")
>>> for var in block.iter_vars():
...     if var.has_role("power"):
...         print(var.name, ': ', var.role)
...
Pmax: 'power'
PSource: 'power.available.source'
PinAvail: 'power.available.input'
PLoad: 'power.delivered.load'
```

The `Var.has_role()` method includes an `exact` parameter that defines whether a match must be exact, or if a subrole should be considered a match as well. By default, `exact=False`, which means that the method will return `True` if the variable has a role that is either an exact match or a subrole. This is why the above example found variables with roles of “power”, “power.available.source”, “power.available.input”, and “power.delivered.load”. If we set `exact=True`, then only variables with an exact match will be found.

```
>>> for var in block.iter_vars():
...     if var.has_role("power", exact=True):
...         print(var.name, ': ', var.role)
...
Pmax: 'power'
```

If you are working with a hierarchical dataset and want to find all variables with a particular role, you can iterate through the `Block` objects using the `Group.iter_blocks` method. For example, if we want to find all variables with a main role of “power” in all blocks in a group, we can do the following:

```
>>> group = pwdt.read_file_as_group("/my_datafile.pwdt")
>>> for block in group.iter_blocks():
...     for var in block.iter_vars():
...         if var.has_role("power"):
...             print(var.name, ': ', var.role)
...
Pmax: 'power'
PSource: 'power.available.source'
PinAvail: 'power.available.input'
PLoad: 'power.delivered.load'
```

<!-- === 来源: api_reference/public_submodules/viz/_autosummary/keysight.pwdatatools.viz.complex_vector_to_str_series.md === -->

# keysight.pwdatatools.viz.complex\_vector\_to\_str\_series[](#keysight-pwdatatools-viz-complex-vector-to-str-series "Link to this heading")

complex\_vector\_to\_str\_series(*data: ndarray | Series*, *form: Literal['rect', 'polar']*) → Series[](#keysight.pwdatatools.viz.complex_vector_to_str_series "Link to this definition")
:   Return a pandas Series of stringified complex numbers in rect or polar form.

    This is useful for creating labels for legends.

    Parameters:
    :   * **data** – A 1D ndarray or pd.Series of complex numbers.
        * **form** – Either `'rect'` or `'polar'`.

    Examples

    ```
    >>> import numpy as np
    >>> import pandas as pd
    >>> from keysight.pwdatatools.viz import complex_vector_to_str_series
    >>> data = np.array([1+1j, 2+2j, 3+3j])
    >>> series = complex_vector_to_str_series(data, form="rect")
    >>> series
    0    1.000 + j 1.000
    1    2.000 + j 2.000
    2    3.000 + j 3.000
    dtype: string
    >>> series = complex_vector_to_str_series(data, form="polar")
    >>> series
    0    1.414 | 45.0
    1    2.828 | 45.0
    2    4.243 | 45.0
    dtype: string
    ```

<!-- === 来源: api_reference/public_submodules/viz/_autosummary/keysight.pwdatatools.viz.contourplot.md === -->

# keysight.pwdatatools.viz.contourplot[](#keysight-pwdatatools-viz-contourplot "Link to this heading")

contourplot(*\**, *x: KeyorVector*, *y: KeyorVector*, *z: KeyorVector*, *ax=None*, *sca: bool = False*, *data: 'VizDataInput' | None = None*, *levels: int | ArrayLike | None = None*, *add\_xlabel: bool = True*, *add\_ylabel: bool = True*, *xlabel: str | None = None*, *ylabel: str | None = None*, *\*\*kwargs*)[](#keysight.pwdatatools.viz.contourplot "Link to this definition")
:   Plot contour lines on a matplotlib Axes.

    Parameters:
    :   * **x** – A str key in data, a 1D ndarray, or pd.Series to use as
          the x variable.
        * **y** – A str key in data, a 1D ndarray, or pd.Series to use as
          the y variable.
        * **z** – A str key in data, a 1D ndarray, or pd.Series that
          represents z variable to contour.
        * **ax** – The matplotlib Axes to plot on. If `None`, use the
          current axes. Defaults to `None`.
        * **sca** – A bool that determines whether to set the Axes used for plotting
          as the current Axes. Invokes `matplotlib.pyplot.sca()`.
          Defaults to `False`.
        * **data** – An optional DataFrame, Block, or mapping with all or some of
          the x, y, and z variables.
        * **levels** (*int or array-like, optional*) – Determines the number and positions of the contour lines or
          regions. If an int, use matplotlib’s MaxNLocator, which tries to
          automatically choose no more than n+1 “nice” contour levels
          between minimum and maximum numeric values of Z. If array-like,
          draw contour lines at the specified levels. The values must be in
          increasing order.
        * **add\_xlabel** – If `True`, add an x label to the Axes. Defaults to `True`.
        * **add\_ylabel** – If `True`, add a y label to the Axes. Defaults to `True`.
        * **xlabel** – If `add_xlabel` is `True`, use this as the x label. If
          `None`, auto-create the label. Auto-creation tries to use the
          name of the x variable. If name of the variable is unknown, use
          the default of label, which is `'x'`. Defaults to `None`.
        * **ylabel** – If `add_ylabel` is `True`, use this as the y label. If
          `None`, auto-create the label. Auto-creation tries to use the
          name of the y variable. If name of the variable is unknown, use
          the default of label, which is `'y'`. Defaults to `None`.
        * **\*\*kwargs** – Additional keyword arguments are passed on to
          `matplotlib.pyplot.contour`. See the matplotlib documentation
          for more info.

    Return type:
    :   matplotlib.contour.QuadContourSet


---

draw\_smith\_chart(*ax=None*, *\**, *sca: bool = False*, *\*\*kwargs*)[](#keysight.pwdatatools.viz.draw_smith_chart "Link to this definition")

# keysight.pwdatatools.viz.make\_contour\_levels[](#keysight-pwdatatools-viz-make-contour-levels "Link to this heading")

make\_contour\_levels(*z: KeyorVector*, *\**, *data: 'VizDataInput' | None = None*, *stepsize: float | int = nan*, *nlevels: int = 10*, *limit: float | int = nan*, *limit\_type: Literal['max', 'min'] = 'max'*)[](#keysight.pwdatatools.viz.make_contour_levels "Link to this definition")
:   Make array of contour levels for use in contour plotting.

    Parameters:
    :   * **z** – The z data. Can be a key to a column in data or a vector
          (a 1D numpy ndarray or pandas Series).
        * **data** – An optional pandas DataFrame, Block, or mapping. If provided,
          x, y, and z can be str keys. Defaults to `None`.
        * **stepsize** – The step size between each contour level. If NaN, use
          auto-calculated step size. Defaults to `numpy.nan`.
        * **nlevels** – The number of contour levels to plot. Defaults to 5.
        * **limit** – A maximum or minimum value for the levels. If NaN, the max or
          min of the gridded data is used. Defaults to `numpy.nan`.
        * **limit\_type** – Either `'max'` or `'min'`. Defaults to `'max'`.

          automatically choose no more than n+1 “nice” contour levels
          between minimum and maximum numeric values of Z. If array-like,
          draw contour lines at the specified levels. The values must be in
          increasing order.
        * **add\_xlabel** – If `True`, add an x label to the Axes. Defaults to `True`.
        * **add\_ylabel** – If `True`, add a y label to the Axes. Defaults to `True`.
        * **xlabel** – If `add_xlabel` is `True`, use this as the x label. If
          `None`, auto-create the label. Auto-creation tries to use the
          name of the x variable. If name of the variable is unknown, use
          the default of label, which is `'x'`. Defaults to `None`.
        * **ylabel** – If `add_ylabel` is `True`, use this as the y label. If
          `None`, auto-create the label. Auto-creation tries to use the
          name of the y variable. If name of the variable is unknown, use
          the default of label, which is `'y'`. Defaults to `None`.
        * **\*\*kwargs** – Additional keyword arguments are passed on to

# keysight.pwdatatools.viz.use\_keysight\_theme[](#keysight-pwdatatools-viz-use-keysight-theme "Link to this heading")

use\_keysight\_theme(*option: Literal['dark', 'light'] = 'light'*)[](#keysight.pwdatatools.viz.use_keysight_theme "Link to this definition")
:   Use Keysight’s color and style theme for matplotlib and seaborn charts.

    Parameters:
    :   **option** (*{‘light’, ‘dark’}, default ‘light’*) – Determines whether to use the dark or light chart backgrounds. The
        dark and light themes also have some other color differences.

# viz[](#viz "Link to this heading")

The `keysight.pwdatatools.viz` module contains functions for plotting and visualiztion. This page summarizes the functions available in this module.
